I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but my mind sort of treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Right away my chest kind of jumps, and I get this strong urge to get back out before I can really think straight. I wasn’t sure if that’s the sort of thing I should come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these episodes of chest jumps and strong urges to escape more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor… it’s happened more than once. I don’t want to make too much of it, but pretty much whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door’s locked, my chest jumps right away and I feel like I need to get back out before I can think straight. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really in a lot of other places, no… I mean, it’s mostly that kind of closed-in situation. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but my mind sort of treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. These days I usually take the stairs if the elevator looks slow, and at the museum I’ll pass on those archive jobs if it means being shut in a little room. If I really can’t avoid a closed-in place, I might ask a friend to stay nearby or wait close, just so I know someone’s there. It feels a bit foolish saying it out loud, but yes, I do avoid them some.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear in these situations related to the idea of being in a closed or enclosed space itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s right, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as really being locked up or anything like that… but when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just sort of treats it like trouble right away. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I wasn’t sure if that’s the sort of thing I should come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these episodes of chest jumps and strong urges to escape more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor… it’s happened more than once. I don’t want to make too much of it, but pretty much whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door’s locked, my chest jumps right away and I feel like I need to get back out before I can think straight. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really in a lot of other places, no… I mean, it’s mostly that kind of closed-in situation. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but my mind sort of treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. These days I usually take the stairs if the elevator looks slow, and at the museum I’ll pass on those archive jobs if it means being shut in a little room. If I really can’t avoid a closed-in place, I might ask a friend to stay nearby or wait close, just so I know someone’s there. It feels a bit foolish saying it out loud, but yes, I do avoid them some.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear in these situations related to the idea of being in a closed or enclosed space itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s right, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as really being locked up or anything like that… but when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just sort of treats it like trouble right away. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack when you are in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, doctor. I don’t usually think, “Oh no, I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that the closed-in feeling bothers me, and I’d rather not be stuck there. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having a panic attack when in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, doctor. I don’t really think ahead like, “I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that I don’t like the closed-in space itself. If I’m saying that right.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern the feeling of panic when in closed spaces or the closed spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s really the closed spaces themselves, doctor. I mean, I know a small storage room or that museum elevator isn’t actually the same as being locked up, but when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s probably nothing too serious, I hope… but I’ve been having trouble when I get into slow elevators or, you know, a small room where the door’s locked. Right away my chest kind of jumps, and I get this strong urge to get back out before I can really think straight. I wasn’t sure if that’s the sort of thing I should come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these episodes of chest jumps and strong urges to escape more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor… it’s happened more than once. I don’t want to make too much of it, but pretty much whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door’s locked, my chest jumps right away and I feel like I need to get back out before I can think straight. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really in a lot of other places, no… I mean, it’s mostly that kind of closed-in situation. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but my mind sort of treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. These days I usually take the stairs if the elevator looks slow, and at the museum I’ll pass on those archive jobs if it means being shut in a little room. If I really can’t avoid a closed-in place, I might ask a friend to stay nearby or wait close, just so I know someone’s there. It feels a bit foolish saying it out loud, but yes, I do avoid them some.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear in these situations related to the idea of being in a closed or enclosed space itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s right, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as really being locked up or anything like that… but when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just sort of treats it like trouble right away. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack when you are in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, doctor. I don’t usually think, “Oh no, I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that the closed-in feeling bothers me, and I’d rather not be stuck there. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having a panic attack when in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, doctor. I don’t really think ahead like, “I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that I don’t like the closed-in space itself. If I’m saying that right.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern the feeling of panic when in closed spaces or the closed spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s really the closed spaces themselves, doctor. I mean, I know a small storage room or that museum elevator isn’t actually the same as being locked up, but when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of having symptoms like a racing heart or feeling trapped that scares you in closed spaces, or is it something else about the spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the space itself, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but once I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just kind of treats it like trouble. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but that’s about how it feels.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.230, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s probably nothing too serious, I hope… but I’ve been having trouble when I get into slow elevators or, you know, a small room where the door’s locked. Right away my chest kind of jumps, and I get this strong urge to get back out before I can really think straight. I wasn’t sure if that’s the sort of thing I should come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these episodes of chest jumps and strong urges to escape more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor… it’s happened more than once. I don’t want to make too much of it, but pretty much whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door’s locked, my chest jumps right away and I feel like I need to get back out before I can think straight. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really in a lot of other places, no… I mean, it’s mostly that kind of closed-in situation. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but my mind sort of treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. These days I usually take the stairs if the elevator looks slow, and at the museum I’ll pass on those archive jobs if it means being shut in a little room. If I really can’t avoid a closed-in place, I might ask a friend to stay nearby or wait close, just so I know someone’s there. It feels a bit foolish saying it out loud, but yes, I do avoid them some.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear in these situations related to the idea of being in a closed or enclosed space itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s right, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as really being locked up or anything like that… but when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just sort of treats it like trouble right away. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack when you are in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, doctor. I don’t usually think, “Oh no, I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that the closed-in feeling bothers me, and I’d rather not be stuck there. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having a panic attack when in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, doctor. I don’t really think ahead like, “I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that I don’t like the closed-in space itself. If I’m saying that right.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern the feeling of panic when in closed spaces or the closed spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s really the closed spaces themselves, doctor. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s probably nothing too serious, I hope… but I’ve been having trouble when I get into slow elevators or, you know, a small room where the door’s locked. Right away my chest kind of jumps, and I get this strong urge to get back out before I can really think straight. I wasn’t sure if that’s the sort of thing I should come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these episodes of chest jumps and strong urges to escape more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor… it’s happened more than once. I don’t want to make too much of it, but pretty much whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door’s locked, my chest jumps right away and I feel like I need to get back out before I can think straight. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really in a lot of other places, no… I mean, it’s mostly that kind of closed-in situation. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but my mind sort of treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. These days I usually take the stairs if the elevator looks slow, and at the museum I’ll pass on those archive jobs if it means being shut in a little room. If I really can’t avoid a closed-in place, I might ask a friend to stay nearby or wait close, just so I know someone’s there. It feels a bit foolish saying it out loud, but yes, I do avoid them some.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear in these situations related to the idea of being in a closed or enclosed space itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s right, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as really being locked up or anything like that… but when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just sort of treats it like trouble right away. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack when you are in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, doctor. I don’t usually think, “Oh no, I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that the closed-in feeling bothers me, and I’d rather not be stuck there. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having a panic attack when in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, doctor. I don’t really think ahead like, “I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that I don’t like the closed-in space itself. If I’m saying that right.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern the feeling of panic when in closed spaces or the closed spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s really the closed spaces themselves, doctor. I mean, I know a small storage room or that museum elevator isn’t actually the same as being locked up, but when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of having symptoms like a racing heart or feeling trapped that scares you in closed spaces, or is it something else about the spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the space itself, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but once I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just kind of treats it like trouble. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but that’s about how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more about what might happen to you when you're in a closed space, like having a panic attack, or is it about being in the closed space?\n\nPatient: It’s more about being in the closed space, I think. It’s more that the place itself sets me off. Like I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really being locked up, but when I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. I wasn’t sure if that’s the sort of thing I should come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these episodes of chest jumps and strong urges to escape more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor… it’s happened more than once. I don’t want to make too much of it, but pretty much whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door’s locked, my chest jumps right away and I feel like I need to get back out before I can think straight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack when you are in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, doctor. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s probably nothing too serious, I hope… but I’ve been having trouble when I get into slow elevators or, you know, a small room where the door’s locked. Right away my chest kind of jumps, and I get this strong urge to get back out before I can really think straight. I wasn’t sure if that’s the sort of thing I should come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these episodes of chest jumps and strong urges to escape more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor… it’s happened more than once. I don’t want to make too much of it, but pretty much whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door’s locked, my chest jumps right away and I feel like I need to get back out before I can think straight. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really in a lot of other places, no… I mean, it’s mostly that kind of closed-in situation. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but my mind sort of treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight. I’m not sure if that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, doctor… at least in those situations. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. These days I usually take the stairs if the elevator looks slow, and at the museum I’ll pass on those archive jobs if it means being shut in a little room. If I really can’t avoid a closed-in place, I might ask a friend to stay nearby or wait close, just so I know someone’s there. It feels a bit foolish saying it out loud, but yes, I do avoid them some.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear in these situations related to the idea of being in a closed or enclosed space itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s right, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as really being locked up or anything like that… but when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just sort of treats it like trouble right away. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack when you are in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, doctor. I don’t usually think, “Oh no, I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that the closed-in feeling bothers me, and I’d rather not be stuck there. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having a panic attack when in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, doctor. I don’t really think ahead like, “I’m going to have a panic attack.” It’s more just that I don’t like the closed-in space itself. If I’m saying that right.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern the feeling of panic when in closed spaces or the closed spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s really the closed spaces themselves, doctor. I mean, I know a small storage room or that museum elevator isn’t actually the same as being locked up, but when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just treats it like trouble. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of having symptoms like a racing heart or feeling trapped that scares you in closed spaces, or is it something else about the spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the space itself, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up, but once I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just kind of treats it like trouble. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but that’s about how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more about what might happen to you when you're in a closed space, like having a panic attack, or is it about being in the closed space?\n\nPatient: It’s more about being in the closed space, I think. I don’t mean to make too much of it, but even a little storage room or an elevator at the museum… I know it’s not the same as being locked up. It’s more that the place itself sets me off. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. When I step into a slow elevator or a small room with the door locked, it comes on right away—my chest sort of jumps, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can really think straight. It’s not something I’m trying to make sound dramatic, but it does hit pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes you're describing could be considered panic attacks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I wouldn’t want to call it the wrong thing. It does feel pretty intense in the moment, but I don’t know if that counts as a panic attack or if it’s just me getting worked up because of the elevator or the locked room. You’d know better than I would, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear or discomfort episodes in situations other than being in slow elevators or small locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of, doctor. It’s really just those closed-in places… like I said, a little storage room or a slow elevator isn’t the same as actually being trapped, I know that. But when there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind kind of treats it like trouble right away. I don’t really get that feeling in other situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid slow elevators or small locked rooms because of the way they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I try not to make a big thing of it. These days I usually take the stairs if the elevator looks slow, and at the museum I’ll pass on those archive jobs if it means being shut in a little room. It feels a bit foolish saying it out loud, but yes, I do avoid them some.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear in these situations related to the idea of being in a closed or enclosed space itself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s right, doctor. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as really being locked up or anything like that… but when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind just sort of treats it like trouble right away. I know it may sound silly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of closed spaces the only thing that causes you anxiety, or is there also a fear of having a panic attack or other symptoms in those situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the closed-in part itself, doctor. I don’t really go in thinking, “What if I have some spell?” or anything like that. It’s more… I know a little storage room or the museum elevator isn’t the same as being locked up, but the minute I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I know that may not make much sense, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack when you are in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, doctor. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of having symptoms like a racing heart or feeling trapped that scares you in closed spaces, or is it something else about the spaces themselves?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the space itself, doctor.
